========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, it's good to meet you too. Um, well, I guess the main reason I’m here is that I’m just having a really, really hard time getting through my work. I’m a secondary school teacher, and obviously, that comes with a lot of grading and lesson planning outside of actual classroom hours. And I really care about my job, I do, but... I’ll set myself up to work, right? I'll make a cup of coffee, clear off my desk at home, and I'll sit down with a stack of essays from my students, or maybe a massive curriculum document the administration wants us to review for the new term. 

I sit down fully intending to work. Like, I am completely ready to just power through it and get it done. But then... I don't even know how it happens, really. Within ten minutes, I'll suddenly sort of snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. I'll be looking right at the words, but I'm actually thinking about three completely different things—like if I need to get my car's oil changed, or a random documentary I watched last week, or what groceries I need to buy later. I'm just barely taking in what’s actually right in front of me. 

And it’s so frustrating because I sit there thinking, "just read the paper, it's right there," but my mind is already a million miles away again before I even finish the sentence. It ends up taking me hours to do what should be a fairly quick task, just sitting there fighting with my own head to read a single page. So, yeah, I just... I can't keep doing it like this, which is why I finally made the appointment.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, actually... it’s funny you ask that, because I've been thinking about that a lot lately. It goes all the way back to, well, even in primary school. My parents kept all my old report cards in this big filing cabinet at their house, and if you go back and read them, it’s almost comical how consistent the comments are. Every single year, different teachers would write the exact same things, like “bright but careless,” or they’d specifically note that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” 

Like, they knew I understood the material, but if the teacher walked away from my desk to help someone else, I'd just drift off into space or start staring at the grain on my desk, just like I do now with my grading. And my parents... oh man, they used to get so frustrated with me about my backpack. They were always, and I mean *always*, finding these old worksheets just completely crumpled up at the very bottom of my bag. Like, shoved under a jacket or crushed under a textbook. I would do the work, or at least start it, and then I’d just completely forget to actually turn it in. 

My mom used to pull out these incredibly wrinkled pieces of paper and try to flatten them out on the kitchen table, just asking me why I couldn't just walk up to the teacher's desk and hand it in when everyone else did. And I never had a good answer for her. It just ceased to exist in my brain the second I put it in my bag. So, yeah, I guess this isn't exactly a new problem for me. It's just... it's a lot harder to manage now that I'm the adult in the classroom and there's nobody there to supervise me and make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, that's a good question. I guess... no, not really. I mean, if it's something I'm really into, it's almost the exact opposite problem, to be honest. Like, if I'm reading a book I actually chose to read—like a really good sci-fi novel, or even just researching something random I got curious about online, like the history of cartography or something—I can do that for hours. 

Actually, it can get kind of ridiculous. My partner gets so annoyed with me sometimes because I'll start doing something I enjoy, like working on this little woodworking project I started in the garage, and I'll just completely lose track of time. I'll go out there at like 2:00 PM on a Saturday, just intending to sand down a piece of wood for twenty minutes, and suddenly it's 7:00 PM, it's pitch black outside, I haven't eaten dinner, and I haven't heard them calling me to come inside because I was just so intensely locked into what I was doing. 

It's like I have two modes: either I can't focus on it for more than ten seconds to save my life, or I'm so hyper-focused on it that a bomb could go off next to me and I wouldn't even flinch. And it's so weird, right? Because you'd think if I can focus that intensely on building a birdhouse, I should be able to focus on grading a tenth-grade history essay. But it just doesn't translate. If the interest isn't there, naturally, organically there... my brain just refuses to engage with it. So, yeah, if I enjoy it, I'm locked in. Maybe too locked in sometimes.

Oh, man. Yeah. Yes, absolutely, it spills over into everything else. It’s not just the grading, though that’s bad enough. Like I mentioned, at work, it’s gotten to the point where my department chair actually had to pull me into her office last month. We had this whole sit-down meeting, which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because I just haven't been following through on things. Like, I’ll forget to input grades into the system by the deadline, or I’ll completely space on submitting the required paperwork for a student’s individualized education plan, which is obviously a huge deal legally. So now she’s put me on this official "support plan," which is really just a polite way of saying they’re watching me closely because I keep dropping the ball. I have to check in with her every Friday just to prove I’ve done the bare minimum administrative tasks, and it just makes me feel like a child. 

And then at home... well, it’s not much better. My wife is amazing, she really is, but she's just reached her breaking point with me lately. It used to be that we’d split the household stuff, you know? Like I’d handle the utilities and the car registration, and she’d do the mortgage and the insurance. But I kept missing due dates. Not because we didn’t have the money, but literally just because I would look at a bill, think, "I need to pay that on Tuesday," put it down on the counter, and then it would just cease to exist in my reality until we got a late notice in the mail. Or she'd ask me to call the plumber, and I'd say yes, and then three days later the sink is still leaking and I haven't even looked up a number. 

So now, she’s basically just taken over all of it. All the bills, all the appointments, all the reminders. She even has this shared calendar app on our phones now where she inputs everything because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of any of it anymore. And I hate it. I hate that she has to take on that mental load because my brain just won't hold onto the information. I feel like I'm failing at work and I'm failing as a partner, all because I just can't seem to keep my focus where it needs to be to just... do the normal, basic things adults are supposed to do.

And my parents... oh man, they used to get so frustrated with me about my backpack. I would do the work, or at least start it, and then I’d just completely forget to actually turn it in. \n\nMy mom used to pull out these incredibly wrinkled pieces of paper and try to flatten them out on the kitchen table, just asking me why I couldn't just walk up to the teacher's desk and hand it in when everyone else did. And I never had a good answer for her. It just ceased to exist in my brain the second I put it in my bag. So, yeah, I guess this isn't exactly a new problem for me. It's just... it's a lot harder to manage now that I'm the adult in the classroom and there's nobody there to supervise me and make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing something you genuinely enjoy, do you still find your mind drifting off like this?\n\nPatient: Um, that's a good question. I guess... no, not really. I mean, if it's something I'm really into, it's almost the exact opposite problem, to be honest. Like, if I'm reading a book I actually chose to read—like a really good sci-fi novel, or even just researching something random I got curious about online, like the history of cartography or something—I can do that for hours. \n\nActually, it can get kind of ridiculous. My partner gets so annoyed with me sometimes because I'll start doing something I enjoy, like working on this little woodworking project I started in the garage, and I'll just completely lose track of time. I'll go out there at like 2:00 PM on a Saturday, just intending to sand down a piece of wood for twenty minutes, and suddenly it's 7:00 PM, it's pitch black outside, I haven't eaten dinner, and I haven't heard them calling me to come inside because I was just so intensely locked into what I was doing. \n\nIt's like I have two modes: either I can't focus on it for more than ten seconds to save my life, or I'm so hyper-focused on it that a bomb could go off next to me and I wouldn't even flinch. And it's so weird, right? Because you'd think if I can focus that intensely on building a birdhouse, I should be able to focus on grading a tenth-grade history essay. But it just doesn't translate. If the interest isn't there, naturally, organically there... my brain just refuses to engage with it. So, yeah, if I enjoy it, I'm locked in. Maybe too locked in sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this same difficulty with attention cause problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yeah. Yes, absolutely, it spills over into everything else. It’s not just the grading, though that’s bad enough. Like I mentioned, at work, it’s gotten to the point where my department chair actually had to pull me into her office last month. We had this whole sit-down meeting, which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because I just haven't been following through on things. Like, I’ll forget to input grades into the system by the deadline, or I’ll completely space on submitting the required paperwork for a student’s individualized education plan, which is obviously a huge deal legally. So now she’s put me on this official \"support plan,\" which is really just a polite way of saying they’re watching me closely because I keep dropping the ball. I have to check in with her every Friday just to prove I’ve done the bare minimum administrative tasks, and it just makes me feel like a child. \n\nAnd then at home... well, it’s not much better. My wife is amazing, she really is, but she's just reached her breaking point with me lately. It used to be that we’d split the household stuff, you know? Like I’d handle the utilities and the car registration, and she’d do the mortgage and the insurance. But I kept missing due dates. Not because we didn’t have the money, but literally just because I would look at a bill, think, \"I need to pay that on Tuesday,\" put it down on the counter, and then it would just cease to exist in my reality until we got a late notice in the mail. Or she'd ask me to call the plumber, and I'd say yes, and then three days later the sink is still leaking and I haven't even looked up a number. \n\nSo now, she’s basically just taken over all of it. All the bills, all the appointments, all the reminders. She even has this shared calendar app on our phones now where she inputs everything because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of any of it anymore. And I hate it. I hate that she has to take on that mental load because my brain just won't hold onto the information. I feel like I'm failing at work and I'm failing as a partner, all because I just can't seem to keep my focus where it needs to be to just... do the normal, basic things adults are supposed to do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F90.0","F41.9","F32.9","F43.9"],"note":"","is_final":false}

I feel like I'm failing at work and I'm failing as a partner, all because I just can't seem to keep my focus where it needs to be to just... do the normal, basic things adults are supposed to do.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you sit down to work and your attention drifts, do you usually feel anxious or worried at that moment?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, anxious? No, not really. I mean, not in the moment when I'm actually drifting off. It's more like... well, it's almost like my brain just quietly changes the channel without me even noticing that the remote was pressed, if that makes sense. Like, I don't feel a sense of dread or worry that pulls me away from the grading. I'm not sitting there thinking about my department chair or the support plan or, you know, stressing about whether I'm going to get fired. 

It's more like... I'll be reading a paragraph about the causes of World War I for the fifteenth time, and then suddenly I'm looking out the window at a squirrel on the fence, and I'm wondering what kind of squirrel it is, and then I'm thinking about how I read somewhere that squirrels plant thousands of trees every year because they forget where they buried their nuts, and then I'm wondering if I could build a squirrel feeder in the garage... and it's all very calm. Almost peaceful, honestly. It's just my brain wandering down this totally random path of thoughts. 

The anxiety only hits later. Like, *much* later. It's when I suddenly snap back to reality and look at the clock and realize that forty-five minutes have gone by and I haven't graded a single paper. *That's* when the panic sets in. That's when my heart starts racing and I get that awful, sinking feeling in my stomach, and I start beating myself up, thinking, "Why do you do this? Why can't you just sit here and do the work like a normal person?" But the actual drifting part? No, there's no worry there at all. It's just... a complete absence of focus on the task at hand. It's like I just float away.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, it's good to meet you too. Um, well, I guess the main reason I’m here is that I’m just having a really, really hard time getting through my work. I’m a secondary school teacher, and obviously, that comes with a lot of grading and lesson planning outside of actual classroom hours. And I really care about my job, I do, but... I’ll set myself up to work, right? I'll make a cup of coffee, clear off my desk at home, and I'll sit down with a stack of essays from my students, or maybe a massive curriculum document the administration wants us to review for the new term. \n\nI sit down fully intending to work. Like, I am completely ready to just power through it and get it done. But then... I don't even know how it happens, really. Within ten minutes, I'll suddenly sort of snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. I'll be looking right at the words, but I'm actually thinking about three completely different things—like if I need to get my car's oil changed, or a random documentary I watched last week, or what groceries I need to buy later. I'm just barely taking in what’s actually right in front of me. \n\nAnd it’s so frustrating because I sit there thinking, \"just read the paper, it's right there,\" but my mind is already a million miles away again before I even finish the sentence. It ends up taking me hours to do what should be a fairly quick task, just sitting there fighting with my own head to read a single page. So, yeah, I just... I can't keep doing it like this, which is why I finally made the appointment.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to childhood, did you have similar problems staying focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually... it’s funny you ask that, because I've been thinking about that a lot lately. It goes all the way back to, well, even in primary school. My parents kept all my old report cards in this big filing cabinet at their house, and if you go back and read them, it’s almost comical how consistent the comments are. Every single year, different teachers would write the exact same things, like “bright but careless,” or they’d specifically note that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” \n\nLike, they knew I understood the material, but if the teacher walked away from my desk to help someone else, I'd just drift off into space or start staring at the grain on my desk, just like I do now with my grading. And my parents... oh man, they used to get so frustrated with me about my backpack. They were always, and I mean *always*, finding these old worksheets just completely crumpled up at the very bottom of my bag. Like, shoved under a jacket or crushed under a textbook. I would do the work, or at least start it, and then I’d just completely forget to actually turn it in. \n\nMy mom used to pull out these incredibly wrinkled pieces of paper and try to flatten them out on the kitchen table, just asking me why I couldn't just walk up to the teacher's desk and hand it in when everyone else did. And I never had a good answer for her. It just ceased to exist in my brain the second I put it in my bag. So, yeah, I guess this isn't exactly a new problem for me. It's just... it's a lot harder to manage now that I'm the adult in the classroom and there's nobody there to supervise me and make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing something you genuinely enjoy, do you still find your mind drifting off like this?\n\nPatient: Um, that's a good question. I guess... no, not really. I mean, if it's something I'm really into, it's almost the exact opposite problem, to be honest. Like, if I'm reading a book I actually chose to read—like a really good sci-fi novel, or even just researching something random I got curious about online, like the history of cartography or something—I can do that for hours. \n\nActually, it can get kind of ridiculous. My partner gets so annoyed with me sometimes because I'll start doing something I enjoy, like working on this little woodworking project I started in the garage, and I'll just completely lose track of time. I'll go out there at like 2:00 PM on a Saturday, just intending to sand down a piece of wood for twenty minutes, and suddenly it's 7:00 PM, it's pitch black outside, I haven't eaten dinner, and I haven't heard them calling me to come inside because I was just so intensely locked into what I was doing. \n\nIt's like I have two modes: either I can't focus on it for more than ten seconds to save my life, or I'm so hyper-focused on it that a bomb could go off next to me and I wouldn't even flinch. And it's so weird, right? Because you'd think if I can focus that intensely on building a birdhouse, I should be able to focus on grading a tenth-grade history essay. But it just doesn't translate. If the interest isn't there, naturally, organically there... my brain just refuses to engage with it. So, yeah, if I enjoy it, I'm locked in. Maybe too locked in sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this same difficulty with attention cause problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yeah. Yes, absolutely, it spills over into everything else. It’s not just the grading, though that’s bad enough. Like I mentioned, at work, it’s gotten to the point where my department chair actually had to pull me into her office last month. We had this whole sit-down meeting, which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because I just haven't been following through on things. Like, I’ll forget to input grades into the system by the deadline, or I’ll completely space on submitting the required paperwork for a student’s individualized education plan, which is obviously a huge deal legally. So now she’s put me on this official \"support plan,\" which is really just a polite way of saying they’re watching me closely because I keep dropping the ball. I have to check in with her every Friday just to prove I’ve done the bare minimum administrative tasks, and it just makes me feel like a child. \n\nAnd then at home... well, it’s not much better. My wife is amazing, she really is, but she's just reached her breaking point with me lately. It used to be that we’d split the household stuff, you know? Like I’d handle the utilities and the car registration, and she’d do the mortgage and the insurance. But I kept missing due dates. Not because we didn’t have the money, but literally just because I would look at a bill, think, \"I need to pay that on Tuesday,\" put it down on the counter, and then it would just cease to exist in my reality until we got a late notice in the mail. Or she'd ask me to call the plumber, and I'd say yes, and then three days later the sink is still leaking and I haven't even looked up a number. \n\nSo now, she’s basically just taken over all of it. All the bills, all the appointments, all the reminders. She even has this shared calendar app on our phones now where she inputs everything because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of any of it anymore. And I hate it. I hate that she has to take on that mental load because my brain just won't hold onto the information. I feel like I'm failing at work and I'm failing as a partner, all because I just can't seem to keep my focus where it needs to be to just... do the normal, basic things adults are supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: When you sit down to work and your attention drifts, do you usually feel anxious or worried at that moment?\n\nPatient: Um, anxious? I have to check in with her every Friday just to prove I’ve done the bare minimum administrative tasks, and it just makes me feel like a child. \n\nAnd then at home... well, it’s not much better. My wife is amazing, she really is, but she's just reached her breaking point with me lately. It used to be that we’d split the household stuff, you know? Like I’d handle the utilities and the car registration, and she’d do the mortgage and the insurance. But I kept missing due dates. Not because we didn’t have the money, but literally just because I would look at a bill, think, \"I need to pay that on Tuesday,\" put it down on the counter, and then it would just cease to exist in my reality until we got a late notice in the mail. Or she'd ask me to call the plumber, and I'd say yes, and then three days later the sink is still leaking and I haven't even looked up a number. \n\nSo now, she’s basically just taken over all of it. All the bills, all the appointments, all the reminders. She even has this shared calendar app on our phones now where she inputs everything because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of any of it anymore. And I hate it. I hate that she has to take on that mental load because my brain just won't hold onto the information. I feel like I'm failing at work and I'm failing as a partner, all because I just can't seem to keep my focus where it needs to be to just... do the normal, basic things adults are supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: When you sit down to work and your attention drifts, do you usually feel anxious or worried at that moment?\n\nPatient: Um, anxious? No, not really. I mean, not in the moment when I'm actually drifting off. It's more like... well, it's almost like my brain just quietly changes the channel without me even noticing that the remote was pressed, if that makes sense. Like, I don't feel a sense of dread or worry that pulls me away from the grading. I'm not sitting there thinking about my department chair or the support plan or, you know, stressing about whether I'm going to get fired. \n\nIt's more like... I'll be reading a paragraph about the causes of World War I for the fifteenth time, and then suddenly I'm looking out the window at a squirrel on the fence, and I'm wondering what kind of squirrel it is, and then I'm thinking about how I read somewhere that squirrels plant thousands of trees every year because they forget where they buried their nuts, and then I'm wondering if I could build a squirrel feeder in the garage... and it's all very calm. Almost peaceful, honestly. It's just my brain wandering down this totally random path of thoughts. \n\nThe anxiety only hits later. Like, *much* later. It's when I suddenly snap back to reality and look at the clock and realize that forty-five minutes have gone by and I haven't graded a single paper. *That's* when the panic sets in. That's when my heart starts racing and I get that awful, sinking feeling in my stomach, and I start beating myself up, thinking, \"Why do you do this? Why can't you just sit here and do the work like a normal person?\" But the actual drifting part? No, there's no worry there at all. It's just... a complete absence of focus on the task at hand. It's like I just float away.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, physically restless? Like, needing to move around? No, not really. I mean, I can sit still just fine. It's funny you ask that, actually, because my mom used to tell me stories about my cousin, David—he’s a few years younger than me—and when he was little, he literally could not sit in a chair for dinner. He’d be bouncing off the walls, running around the table, tapping his fork on everything. They had to get him one of those special wobble stools for school just so he wouldn't disrupt the whole class. But I was never like that. I was the quiet kid in the back of the room, you know? 

Even now, sitting at my desk to grade or whatever, my body is perfectly still. I'm not fidgeting or tapping my foot or feeling like I need to get up and run a marathon. I can sit in one spot for hours, especially if I'm doing something I like, like that woodworking I mentioned. My body just stays put. It’s entirely in my head. My brain is what’s doing the running around, jumping from the grading to the squirrel to the history of cartography. So, yeah, physically, I'm pretty stationary. I don't get that urge to move or that feeling like I have a motor running inside me. It's just my thoughts that won't stay in their seat, if that makes sense.

Every single year, different teachers would write the exact same things, like “bright but careless,” or they’d specifically note that I “doesn’t finish work unless closely supervised.” \n\nLike, they knew I understood the material, but if the teacher walked away from my desk to help someone else, I'd just drift off into space or start staring at the grain on my desk, just like I do now with my grading. They were always, and I mean *always*, finding these old worksheets just completely crumpled up at the very bottom of my bag. Like, shoved under a jacket or crushed under a textbook. I would do the work, or at least start it, and then I’d just completely forget to actually turn it in. \n\nMy mom used to pull out these incredibly wrinkled pieces of paper and try to flatten them out on the kitchen table, just asking me why I couldn't just walk up to the teacher's desk and hand it in when everyone else did. And I never had a good answer for her. It just ceased to exist in my brain the second I put it in my bag. So, yeah, I guess this isn't exactly a new problem for me. It's just... it's a lot harder to manage now that I'm the adult in the classroom and there's nobody there to supervise me and make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you’re doing something you genuinely enjoy, do you still find your mind drifting off like this?\n\nPatient: Um, that's a good question. I guess... no, not really. I mean, if it's something I'm really into, it's almost the exact opposite problem, to be honest. Like, if I'm reading a book I actually chose to read—like a really good sci-fi novel, or even just researching something random I got curious about online, like the history of cartography or something—I can do that for hours. \n\nActually, it can get kind of ridiculous. My partner gets so annoyed with me sometimes because I'll start doing something I enjoy, like working on this little woodworking project I started in the garage, and I'll just completely lose track of time. I'll go out there at like 2:00 PM on a Saturday, just intending to sand down a piece of wood for twenty minutes, and suddenly it's 7:00 PM, it's pitch black outside, I haven't eaten dinner, and I haven't heard them calling me to come inside because I was just so intensely locked into what I was doing. \n\nIt's like I have two modes: either I can't focus on it for more than ten seconds to save my life, or I'm so hyper-focused on it that a bomb could go off next to me and I wouldn't even flinch. And it's so weird, right? Because you'd think if I can focus that intensely on building a birdhouse, I should be able to focus on grading a tenth-grade history essay. But it just doesn't translate. If the interest isn't there, naturally, organically there... my brain just refuses to engage with it. So, yeah, if I enjoy it, I'm locked in. Maybe too locked in sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, does this same difficulty with attention cause problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yeah. Yes, absolutely, it spills over into everything else. It’s not just the grading, though that’s bad enough. Like I mentioned, at work, it’s gotten to the point where my department chair actually had to pull me into her office last month. We had this whole sit-down meeting, which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because I just haven't been following through on things. Like, I’ll forget to input grades into the system by the deadline, or I’ll completely space on submitting the required paperwork for a student’s individualized education plan, which is obviously a huge deal legally. So now she’s put me on this official \"support plan,\" which is really just a polite way of saying they’re watching me closely because I keep dropping the ball. I have to check in with her every Friday just to prove I’ve done the bare minimum administrative tasks, and it just makes me feel like a child. \n\nAnd then at home... well, it’s not much better. My wife is amazing, she really is, but she's just reached her breaking point with me lately. It used to be that we’d split the household stuff, you know? Like I’d handle the utilities and the car registration, and she’d do the mortgage and the insurance. But I kept missing due dates. Not because we didn’t have the money, but literally just because I would look at a bill, think, \"I need to pay that on Tuesday,\" put it down on the counter, and then it would just cease to exist in my reality until we got a late notice in the mail. Or she'd ask me to call the plumber, and I'd say yes, and then three days later the sink is still leaking and I haven't even looked up a number. \n\nSo now, she’s basically just taken over all of it. All the bills, all the appointments, all the reminders. She even has this shared calendar app on our phones now where she inputs everything because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of any of it anymore. And I hate it. I hate that she has to take on that mental load because my brain just won't hold onto the information. I feel like I'm failing at work and I'm failing as a partner, all because I just can't seem to keep my focus where it needs to be to just... do the normal, basic things adults are supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: When you sit down to work and your attention drifts, do you usually feel anxious or worried at that moment?\n\nPatient: Um, anxious? No, not really. I mean, not in the moment when I'm actually drifting off. It's more like... well, it's almost like my brain just quietly changes the channel without me even noticing that the remote was pressed, if that makes sense. Like, I don't feel a sense of dread or worry that pulls me away from the grading. I'm not sitting there thinking about my department chair or the support plan or, you know, stressing about whether I'm going to get fired. \n\nIt's more like... I'll be reading a paragraph about the causes of World War I for the fifteenth time, and then suddenly I'm looking out the window at a squirrel on the fence, and I'm wondering what kind of squirrel it is, and then I'm thinking about how I read somewhere that squirrels plant thousands of trees every year because they forget where they buried their nuts, and then I'm wondering if I could build a squirrel feeder in the garage... and it's all very calm. Almost peaceful, honestly. It's just my brain wandering down this totally random path of thoughts. \n\nThe anxiety only hits later. Like, *much* later. It's when I suddenly snap back to reality and look at the clock and realize that forty-five minutes have gone by and I haven't graded a single paper. *That's* when the panic sets in. That's when my heart starts racing and I get that awful, sinking feeling in my stomach, and I start beating myself up, thinking, \"Why do you do this? Why can't you just sit here and do the work like a normal person?\" But the actual drifting part? No, there's no worry there at all. It's just... a complete absence of focus on the task at hand. It's like I just float away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: Um, physically restless? Like, needing to move around? No, not really. I mean, I can sit still just fine. It's funny you ask that, actually, because my mom used to tell me stories about my cousin, David—he’s a few years younger than me—and when he was little, he literally could not sit in a chair for dinner. He’d be bouncing off the walls, running around the table, tapping his fork on everything. They had to get him one of those special wobble stools for school just so he wouldn't disrupt the whole class. But I was never like that. I was the quiet kid in the back of the room, you know? \n\nEven now, sitting at my desk to grade or whatever, my body is perfectly still. I'm not fidgeting or tapping my foot or feeling like I need to get up and run a marathon. I can sit in one spot for hours, especially if I'm doing something I like, like that woodworking I mentioned. My body just stays put. It’s entirely in my head. My brain is what’s doing the running around, jumping from the grading to the squirrel to the history of cartography. So, yeah, physically, I'm pretty stationary. I don't get that urge to move or that feeling like I have a motor running inside me. It's just my thoughts that won't stay in their seat, if that makes sense."
  }
]

Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHi, yeah, it's good to meet you too. Um, well, I guess the main reason I’m here is that I’m just having a really, really hard time getting through my work. I’m a secondary school teacher, and obviously, that comes with a lot of grading and lesson planning outside of actual classroom hours. And I really care about my job, I do, but... I’ll set myself up to work, right? I'll make a cup of coffee, clear off my desk at home, and I'll sit down with a stack of essays from my students, or maybe a massive curriculum document the administration wants us to review for the new term. \n\nI sit down fully intending to work. Like, I am completely ready to just power through it and get it done. But then... I don't even know how it happens, really. Within ten minutes, I'll suddenly sort of snap back to reality and realize I’ve just been staring at the exact same page the entire time. I'll be looking right at the words, but I'm actually thinking about three completely different things—like if I need to get my car's oil changed, or a random documentary I watched last week, or what groceries I need to buy later. I'm just barely taking in what’s actually right in front of me. \n\nAnd it’s so frustrating because I sit there thinking, \"just read the paper, it's right there,\" but my mind is already a million miles away again before I even finish the sentence. It ends up taking me hours to do what should be a fairly quick task, just sitting there fighting with my own head to read a single page. So, yeah, I just... I can't keep doing it like this, which is why I finally made the appointment.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. So now she’s put me on this official \"support plan,\" which is really just a polite way of saying they’re watching me closely because I keep dropping the ball. I have to check in with her every Friday just to prove I’ve done the bare minimum administrative tasks, and it just makes me feel like a child. \n\nAnd then at home... well, it’s not much better. My wife is amazing, she really is, but she's just reached her breaking point with me lately. It used to be that we’d split the household stuff, you know? Like I’d handle the utilities and the car registration, and she’d do the mortgage and the insurance. But I kept missing due dates. Not because we didn’t have the money, but literally just because I would look at a bill, think, \"I need to pay that on Tuesday,\" put it down on the counter, and then it would just cease to exist in my reality until we got a late notice in the mail. Or she'd ask me to call the plumber, and I'd say yes, and then three days later the sink is still leaking and I haven't even looked up a number. \n\nSo now, she’s basically just taken over all of it. All the bills, all the appointments, all the reminders. She even has this shared calendar app on our phones now where she inputs everything because she said she just can’t count on me to keep track of any of it anymore. And I hate it. I hate that she has to take on that mental load because my brain just won't hold onto the information. I feel like I'm failing at work and I'm failing as a partner, all because I just can't seem to keep my focus where it needs to be to just... do the normal, basic things adults are supposed to do.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nUm, anxious? No, not really. I mean, not in the moment when I'm actually drifting off. It's more like... well, it's almost like my brain just quietly changes the channel without me even noticing that the remote was pressed, if that makes sense. Like, I don't feel a sense of dread or worry that pulls me away from the grading. I'm not sitting there thinking about my department chair or the support plan or, you know, stressing about whether I'm going to get fired. \n\nIt's more like... I'll be reading a paragraph about the causes of World War I for the fifteenth time, and then suddenly I'm looking out the window at a squirrel on the fence, and I'm wondering what kind of squirrel it is, and then I'm thinking about how I read somewhere that squirrels plant thousands of trees every year because they forget where they buried their nuts, and then I'm wondering if I could build a squirrel feeder in the garage... and it's all very calm. Almost peaceful, honestly. It's just my brain wandering down this totally random path of thoughts. \n\nThe anxiety only hits later. Like, *much* later. It's when I suddenly snap back to reality and look at the clock and realize that forty-five minutes have gone by and I haven't graded a single paper. *That's* when the panic sets in. That's when my heart starts racing and I get that awful, sinking feeling in my stomach, and I start beating myself up, thinking, \"Why do you do this? Why can't you just sit here and do the work like a normal person?\" But the actual drifting part? No, there's no worry there at all. It's just... a complete absence of focus on the task at hand. It's like I just float away.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
